## Model Solution

Below is a self‑contained, fully detailed proof of the Koebe–Bieberbach radius theorem (the “quarter‑theorem”) together with the auxiliary results (a)–(g) that are required in the problem statement.  
All arguments are elementary and rely only on the area theorem (Gronwall’s area theorem) and elementary complex analysis.

---------------------------------------------------------------------

### 1.  Notation and preliminaries  

* \(\mathbb D=\{z\in\mathbb C:|z|<1\}\) denotes the unit disc.  
* A holomorphic map \(f:\mathbb D\to\mathbb C\) is **normalized** if \(f(0)=0\) and \(f'(0)=1\).  
* A map \(f\) is **univalent** (injective) on \(\mathbb D\) if it is one‑to‑one.  
* For a holomorphic function \(F\) we write its Laurent expansion at the origin as  
  \[
  F(z)=\frac{c_{-1}}{z}+c_0+c_1z+c_2z^2+\cdots .
  \]

The **area theorem** (Gronwall, 1913) says:

> If \(F\) is univalent in \(\mathbb D\) and has a simple pole at the origin,
> \[
> F(z)=\frac1z+c_0+c_1z+c_2z^2+\cdots ,
> \]
> then for every \(0<\rho<1\)
> \[
> \operatorname{area}\Bigl(\mathbb C\setminus F(\mathbb D_\rho)\Bigr)
>   =\pi\Bigl(\frac1{\rho^{2}}-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}n|c_n|^{2}\rho^{2n}\Bigr)\ge0 .
> \]
> Consequently
> \[
> \sum_{n=1}^{\infty}n|c_n|^{2}\rho^{2n}\le\frac1{\rho^{2}}\qquad(0<\rho<1).
> \tag{1}
> \]

Taking \(\rho\to1^{-}\) we obtain the **area inequality**
> \[
> \sum_{n=1}^{\infty}n|c_n|^{2}\le1 .
> \tag{2}
> \]

---------------------------------------------------------------------

### 2.  Part (a) – No uniform radius without any extra hypothesis  

**Claim.** There is no number \(r>0\) such that for *every* holomorphic \(f\) with \(f(0)=0\) we have \(D_r(0)\subset f(\mathbb D)\).

**Proof.** For each integer \(n\ge1\) define
\[
f_n(z)=\frac{z}{n}\qquad(z\in\mathbb D).
\]
Each \(f_n\) is holomorphic, \(f_n(0)=0\), and
\[
f_n'(0)=\frac1n\longrightarrow0 .
\]
Moreover,
\[
f_n(\mathbb D)=\Bigl\{\frac{z}{n}:|z|<1\Bigr\}=D_{1/n}(0),
\]
so the point \(1/n\) is **not** in the image of \(f_n\).  

If a universal radius \(r>0\) existed, pick \(n\) so large that \(1/n<r\). Then
\(1/n\in D_r(0)\) but \(1/n\notin f_n(\mathbb D)\), a contradiction. ∎



---------------------------------------------------------------------

### 3.  Part (b) – Even the condition \(f'(0)=1\) does not help  

**Claim.** There is no \(r>0\) such that for every holomorphic \(f\) with \(f(0)=0\) and \(f'(0)=1\) we have \(D_r(0)\subset f(\mathbb D)\).

**Proof.** For a parameter \(\varepsilon>0\) set
\[
f_\varepsilon(z)=\varepsilon\bigl(e^{z/\varepsilon}-1\bigr),\qquad z\in\mathbb D .
\]
Then \(f_\varepsilon(0)=0\) and
\[
f_\varepsilon'(z)=\varepsilon\cdot\frac1\varepsilon e^{z/\varepsilon}=e^{z/\varepsilon},
\qquad\text{hence }f_\varepsilon'(0)=1 .
\]

Because \(e^{z/\varepsilon}\neq0\) for any \(z\), we have
\[
f_\varepsilon(z)=-\varepsilon\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad e^{z/\varepsilon}=0,
\]
which never occurs. Thus the value \(-\varepsilon\) is omitted from the image of \(f_\varepsilon\).

If a universal radius \(r\) existed, choose \(\varepsilon<r\). Then
\(-\varepsilon\in D_r(0)\) but \(-\varepsilon\notin f_\varepsilon(\mathbb D)\), a contradiction. ∎



---------------------------------------------------------------------

### 4.  Part (c) – The area inequality for a univalent function with a pole  

Let
\[
h(z)=\frac1z+c_0+c_1z+c_2z^2+\cdots ,\qquad 0<|
